First pending source unit: Sirach Prologue 1. Never-reviewed source units start at Sirach 34:1. Deferred source evidence.

Sirach 1:1

All wisdom is from the Lord and is with Him for ever.

  1. Proverbs 2:6 Wisdom teaching parallel

    Sirach locates all wisdom with the Lord; Proverbs likewise names the Lord as giver of wisdom and the source of knowledge and understanding.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 2:6 For the Lord gives wisdom, and from His presence come knowledge and understanding.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:2

Who can count the sand of the seas, the drops of rain, and the days of eternity?

  1. Job 38:37 Rhetorical image parallel

    Sirach asks who can count rain and other quantities beyond human reckoning; God's question in Job asks who counts clouds with wisdom. Both challenge human mastery of the heavens' quantities.

    Read matching passage

    Job 38:37 Who counts clouds with wisdom and inclined heaven toward earth?

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:3

Who can trace the height of heaven, the breadth of the earth, the abyss, and wisdom?

  1. Job 11:7-9 Rhetorical image parallel

    Sirach links inaccessible cosmic dimensions with wisdom. The speech in Job likewise challenges human understanding through heaven's height, depths, and the earth and sea's extent; its accusation against Job is not endorsed here.

    Read matching passage

    Job 11:7 Will you find a trace of the Lord, or have you reached the furthest things the Almighty made?

    Job 11:8 Heaven is high—what will you do? What do you know of things deeper than those in Hades,

    Job 11:9 or longer than the measure of earth, or than the breadth of the sea?

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:4

Wisdom was created before all things, and discerning understanding from eternity.

  1. Proverbs 8:22-25 Personified wisdom parallel

    Wisdom's creation before all things corresponds to personified Wisdom's account of being created before earth, depths and mountains in Proverbs. The shared claim concerns wisdom's priority in creation.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 8:22 The Lord created me as the beginning of His ways, for His works.

    Proverbs 8:23 Before the age He founded me, in the beginning,

    Proverbs 8:24 before making the earth and before making the deeps, before the springs of the waters came forth,

    Proverbs 8:25 before the mountains were established, and before all hills He gives me birth.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:6

To whom was the root of wisdom revealed? Who knew her skillful works?

  1. Job 28:20-23 Wisdom teaching parallel

    Sirach asks to whom wisdom's root was revealed; Job asks where wisdom is found, says it escapes human sight, and contrasts this with God's knowledge of its place.

    Read matching passage

    Job 28:20 But where was wisdom found, and what is the place of understanding?

    Job 28:21 It has escaped every human and was hidden from the birds of heaven.

    Job 28:22 Destruction and Death said, ‘We have heard its renown.’

    Job 28:23 God established its way well, and He knows its place.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:8

There is One who is wise, greatly to be feared, seated upon His throne,

  1. Romans 16:27 Divine attribute parallel

    Sirach identifies One who is wise, subsequently named as the Lord; Romans' doxology praises the only wise God. The connection concerns divine wisdom, without adding throne imagery to Romans.

    Read matching passage

    Romans 16:27 to the only wise God, through Jesus Christ, to whom be the glory throughout the ages. Amen.]

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:9

the Lord Himself. He created her, saw her, counted her out, and poured her upon all His works,

  1. Job 28:27 Personified wisdom parallel

    Sirach describes God seeing and ordering wisdom before pouring her upon His works; Job likewise describes Him seeing, preparing and tracing out wisdom within His ordering of creation.

    Read matching passage

    Job 28:27 Then He saw wisdom and expounded it; having prepared it, He traced it out.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:10

with all flesh according to His giving; He supplied her to those who love Him.

  1. James 1:5 Wisdom as divine gift

    Wisdom is supplied according to God's giving in Sirach; James directs those lacking wisdom to the God who gives generously. Both locate the gift's availability in God.

    Read matching passage

    James 1:5 But if any of you lacks wisdom, let him ask God, who gives to all generously and without reproach, and it will be given to him.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:11

The fear of the Lord is glory and a boast, gladness and a crown of rejoicing.

  1. Psalms 127:1-2 Reverence and gladness

    Sirach associates fear of the Lord with rejoicing; the psalm calls those fearing the Lord happy and promises their well-being. Reverence and happiness are joined in both.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 127:1 A song of the ascents. Happy are all who fear the Lord, who walk in His ways.

    Psalms 127:2 You will eat the labors of your wrists; happy are you, and it will be well with you.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:12

The fear of the Lord will delight the heart and give gladness, joy, and length of days.

  1. Proverbs 10:27 Reverence and longevity

    Sirach promises length of days through fear of the Lord; Proverbs explicitly says that fear of the Lord adds days. The longevity promise closely corresponds.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 10:27 The fear of the Lord adds days, but the years of the impious will become few.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:13

It will go well at the end with the one who fears the Lord, and on the day of his death he will be blessed.

  1. Ecclesiastes 8:12 Reverence and well-being

    Sirach says the Lord-fearing person's end will be good; Ecclesiastes affirms that it will be good for those fearing God despite the sinner's apparent prolongation.

    Read matching passage

    Ecclesiastes 8:12 The sinner has done evil from then, and there is length for him; for I know that it will be good for those who fear God, so that they may fear before Him.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:14

To fear the Lord is the beginning of wisdom; she was created together with the faithful in the womb.

  1. Proverbs 9:10 Verbal wisdom parallel

    Both verses explicitly identify fear of the Lord as the beginning of wisdom. Sirach's additional womb imagery is not attributed to Proverbs.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 9:10 The beginning of wisdom is the fear of the Lord, and the counsel of holy ones is understanding.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:15

Among human beings she nested on an eternal foundation, and among their offspring she will be trusted.

  1. Sirach 4:16 Generational wisdom parallel

    Wisdom is trusted among people's offspring in Sirach 1; chapter 4 explains her possession as an inheritance held by descendants. Both describe wisdom continuing across generations.

    Read matching passage

    Sirach 4:16 If he trusts, he will possess her as an inheritance, and his descendants will hold her in possession.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:16

To fear the Lord is the fullness of wisdom, and she intoxicates them with her fruits.

  1. Sirach 24:19-21 Personified wisdom imagery

    Wisdom intoxicates with her fruits in chapter 1; in chapter 24 she invites people to eat her produce and drink. Both portray participation in wisdom through consumption imagery.

    Read matching passage

    Sirach 24:19 Come to me, you who desire me, and be filled from my produce.

    Sirach 24:20 For my memory is sweeter than honey, and my inheritance than a honeycomb.

    Sirach 24:21 Those who eat me will still hunger, and those who drink me will still thirst.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:17

She will fill their whole house with desirable things and their storehouses with her produce.

  1. Proverbs 24:3-4 Wisdom and household abundance

    Sirach's wisdom fills a house and storehouses with desirable produce; Proverbs joins a house built through wisdom to store-rooms filled with precious wealth through discernment.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 24:3 With wisdom a house is built, and with understanding it is raised upright;

    Proverbs 24:4 with discernment store-rooms are filled from every precious and good wealth.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:18

The fear of the Lord is the crown of wisdom, making peace and the health of healing flourish.

  1. Proverbs 3:7-8 Reverence and healing

    Sirach connects fear of the Lord with healing health; Proverbs connects fearing God and avoiding evil with healing for body and bones. Both describe reverence through bodily well-being.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 3:7 Do not be prudent in your own estimation; fear God and turn away from every evil.

    Proverbs 3:8 Then there will be healing for your body and care for your bones.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:19

He saw her and counted her out; He rained down skill and discerning knowledge and raised high the glory of those who hold her fast.

  1. Job 28:27 Divine ordering of wisdom

    Sirach's divine seeing and distribution of wisdom corresponds to Job's account of God seeing, preparing and expounding wisdom. Job's wording is not represented as an identical counting formula.

    Read matching passage

    Job 28:27 Then He saw wisdom and expounded it; having prepared it, He traced it out.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:20

To fear the Lord is the root of wisdom, and her branches are length of days.

  1. Proverbs 10:27 Reverence and longevity

    Sirach pictures long days as branches growing from fear of the Lord; Proverbs states directly that fear of the Lord adds days. The image develops the same longevity teaching.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 10:27 The fear of the Lord adds days, but the years of the impious will become few.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:22

Unjust anger cannot be justified, for the weight of a man’s anger brings about his fall.

  1. James 1:19-20 Anger and righteousness

    Sirach denies justification to unjust anger; James says human anger does not accomplish God's righteousness. Both reject anger as a reliable means of right conduct.

    Read matching passage

    James 1:19 Know this, my beloved brothers: let every person be quick to hear, slow to speak, slow to anger,

    James 1:20 for a man’s anger does not accomplish the righteousness of God.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:23

A patient person will hold out until the right time, and afterward gladness will spring up for him.

  1. James 5:11 Endurance and its outcome

    Sirach promises gladness after patient endurance; James calls those who endure blessed and invokes Job's outcome from the compassionate Lord. Both look beyond present hardship.

    Read matching passage

    James 5:11 Look, we call those who endured blessed. You heard of Job’s endurance and saw the outcome from the Lord, because the Lord is full of compassion and merciful.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:24

Until the right time he will conceal his words, and the lips of many will recount his understanding.

  1. Proverbs 17:27-28 Wise restraint of speech

    Sirach's wise person conceals words until the right time; Proverbs commends withholding a harsh word and associates silence with intelligence. Speech restraint displays discernment in both.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 17:27 One who refrains from sending forth a harsh word is discerning, and a patient man is intelligent.

    Proverbs 17:28 Wisdom will be reckoned to a senseless person who asks for wisdom, and someone who makes himself mute will seem intelligent.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:25

In wisdom’s treasuries are sayings of knowledge, but reverence for God is an abomination to a sinner.

  1. Proverbs 1:7 Wisdom and rejected reverence

    Sirach contrasts wisdom's knowledge with the sinner's aversion to reverence; Proverbs joins reverence with perception and says the ungodly despise wisdom and discipline.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 1:7 The fear of God is the beginning of wisdom, and good understanding belongs to all who practice it. Reverence toward God is the beginning of perception, but the ungodly will despise wisdom and discipline.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:26

If you desire wisdom, keep the commandments, and the Lord will supply her to you.

  1. Psalms 110:10 Practiced reverence and understanding

    Sirach tells wisdom-seekers to keep commandments; the psalm joins fear of the Lord with wisdom and good understanding for those who practice it. Both connect understanding with obedient practice.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 110:10 The beginning of wisdom is the fear of the Lord; good understanding belongs to all who practice it. His praise remains for an age of an age.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:27

For wisdom and instruction are the fear of the Lord, and faithfulness and gentleness are His good pleasure.

  1. James 3:17 Wisdom's moral character

    Sirach associates wisdom with faithfulness and gentleness pleasing to God; James describes wisdom from above as peaceable, considerate and merciful. Both describe wisdom through conduct toward others.

    Read matching passage

    James 3:17 But the wisdom from above is first pure, then peaceable, considerate, open to persuasion, full of mercy and good fruits, impartial, without hypocrisy.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:28

Do not disobey the fear of the Lord, and do not approach Him with a divided heart.

  1. James 4:8 Undivided approach to God

    Sirach forbids approaching God with a divided heart; James commands those of divided mind to purify their hearts as they draw near to God.

    Read matching passage

    James 4:8 Draw near to God, and He will draw near to you. Cleanse your hands, sinners, and purify your hearts, you of divided mind.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:29

Do not play the hypocrite in people’s talk, and keep watch over your lips.

  1. Matthew 23:27-28 Hypocrisy condemned

    Sirach forbids hypocritical conduct in people's speech; Jesus condemns an outward appearance of righteousness concealing inward hypocrisy. Both reject a deceptive public presentation of one's character.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 23:27 Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you resemble whitewashed tombs, which outwardly appear beautiful, but inwardly are full of dead people’s bones and all uncleanness!

    Matthew 23:28 So you too outwardly appear righteous to people, but inwardly you are full of hypocrisy and lawlessness.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 1:30

Do not exalt yourself, lest you fall and bring dishonor upon your soul. The Lord will uncover your hidden things and cast you down in the midst of the assembly, because you did not approach the fear of the Lord and your heart is full of deceit.

  1. Luke 14:7-11 Self-exaltation and public shame

    Sirach warns that self-exaltation ends in humiliation before the assembly; Jesus' banquet parable depicts shame before fellow guests and concludes that self-exalters will be humbled.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 14:7 He told a parable to those who had been invited, noticing how they chose the foremost places at table. He said to them,

    Luke 14:8 “When someone invites you to a wedding feast, do not recline in the foremost place. Someone more honored than you may have been invited by him,

    Luke 14:9 and the one who invited you both will come and say to you, ‘Give this man your place.’ Then you will begin, with shame, to occupy the last place.

    Luke 14:10 But when you are invited, go and recline in the last place, so that when the one who invited you comes, he will say to you, ‘Friend, move up higher.’ Then you will have honor before all who recline at table with you.

    Luke 14:11 For everyone who exalts himself will be humbled, and the one who humbles himself will be exalted.”

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:1

Child, if you come to serve the Lord, prepare your soul for testing.

  1. James 1:2-4 Testing in faithful service

    Sirach prepares a new servant of the Lord for testing; James tells believers to expect trials that test faith and produce endurance. Testing belongs within faithful life in both.

    Read matching passage

    James 1:2 Count it all joy, my brothers, whenever you encounter trials of various kinds,

    James 1:3 knowing that the testing of your faith produces endurance.

    James 1:4 And let endurance have its complete work, so that you may be complete and whole, lacking in nothing.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:2

Set your heart straight and endure; do not rush in a time of calamity.

  1. Psalms 26:14 Steadfast heart in distress

    Sirach commands a steady heart and endurance in calamity; the psalm tells the waiting worshipper to act courageously and let the heart be strong while waiting for the Lord.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 26:14 Wait for the Lord; act courageously, and let your heart be strong, and wait for the Lord.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:3

Cling to Him and do not withdraw, so that you may grow in your last days.

  1. Deuteronomy 10:20 Clinging to the Lord

    Sirach's instruction to cling to the Lord without withdrawing closely corresponds to Deuteronomy's combined commands to fear, serve and cleave to Him.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 10:20 You shall fear the Lord your God, and you shall serve Him, and you shall cleave unto Him, and you shall swear by His name.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:4

Accept whatever is brought upon you, and be patient amid the changes of your humiliation.

  1. Hebrews 12:5-11 Enduring humbling hardship

    Sirach calls for patience in humiliation; Hebrews urges endurance of painful discipline for its later benefit. The comparison concerns patient reception of hardship, without identifying every adversity as the same event.

    Read matching passage

    Hebrews 12:5 and you have forgotten the encouragement that addresses you as sons: “My son, do not make light of the Lord’s discipline, nor faint when reproved by Him.

    Hebrews 12:6 For the Lord disciplines the one He loves, and scourges every son He receives.”

    Hebrews 12:7 Endure for discipline. God deals with you as sons; for what son is there whom a father does not discipline?

    Hebrews 12:8 But if you are without discipline, in which all have become partakers, then you are illegitimate children and not sons.

    Hebrews 12:9 Furthermore, we had our fathers in the flesh to discipline us, and we respected them. But shall we not much more submit to the Father of spirits and live?

    Hebrews 12:10 For they disciplined us for a few days according to what seemed right to them, but He for our benefit, so that we may share in His holiness.

    Hebrews 12:11 Now every discipline seems for the present to be a matter of sorrow, not joy, but afterward it yields the peaceful fruit of righteousness to those trained through it.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:5

For gold is tested in fire, and acceptable people in the furnace of humiliation.

  1. 1 Peter 1:6-7 Testing by fire imagery

    Sirach compares acceptable people tested in humiliation with gold tested in fire; Peter likewise compares faith tested through trials with fire-tested gold. Both use refining to explain human testing.

    Read matching passage

    1 Peter 1:6 In this you rejoice, though now, for a little while, if necessary, you have been grieved by various trials,

    1 Peter 1:7 so that the tested genuineness of your faith—more precious than gold that perishes, though tested by fire—may be found to result in praise and glory and honor at the revelation of Jesus Christ.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:6

Trust Him, and He will support you. Set your ways straight and hope in Him.

  1. Proverbs 3:5-6 Trust and straight paths

    Sirach combines trust in God with setting one's ways straight; Proverbs commands wholehearted trust in God and attendance to wisdom for straight paths, rather than self-reliance.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 3:5 Trust God with your whole heart; do not exalt yourself in your own wisdom.

    Proverbs 3:6 In all your ways acknowledge her, that she may cut your ways straight; and your foot will surely not stumble.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:7

You who fear the Lord, wait for His mercy, and do not turn aside, lest you fall.

  1. Psalms 32:18-22 Waiting for divine mercy

    Sirach addresses those fearing the Lord and waiting for mercy; the psalm describes precisely those who fear Him, hope in His mercy and wait for His help.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 32:18 Behold, the Lord’s eyes are upon those who fear Him, those who hope in His mercy,

    Psalms 32:19 to rescue their souls from death and nourish them in famine.

    Psalms 32:20 Our soul waits for the Lord, for He is our helper and defender.

    Psalms 32:21 For our heart will rejoice in Him, and we have hoped in His holy name.

    Psalms 32:22 May Thy mercy, Lord, be upon us, just as we have hoped in Thee.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:8

You who fear the Lord, trust Him, and your reward will certainly not fail.

  1. Psalms 24:2-3 Trust that will not fail

    Sirach assures the Lord-fearing that trust will not lose its reward; the psalm declares that those waiting for God will not be put to shame. Both oppose trust to final disappointment.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 24:2 In Thee I have trusted; may I not be put to shame, and let my enemies not laugh at me.

    Psalms 24:3 For indeed, all who wait for Thee will certainly not be put to shame; let all who act lawlessly without cause be ashamed.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:9

You who fear the Lord, hope for good things, for eternal gladness and mercy.

  1. Romans 15:13 Hope and believing joy

    Sirach urges hope for gladness and mercy; Romans asks the God of hope to fill believers with joy and peace so that hope abounds. Both join reliance on God with joy.

    Read matching passage

    Romans 15:13 And may the God of hope fill you with all joy and peace in believing, so that you may abound in hope in the power of the Holy Spirit.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:10

Look at the generations of old and see: Who trusted in the Lord and was put to shame? Who remained in His fear and was abandoned? Who called upon Him and was disregarded by Him?

  1. Psalms 21:5-6 Ancestral testimony of trust

    Sirach asks whether earlier generations trusted and were ashamed or called without response; the psalm says the fathers hoped, cried to God, were rescued and were not ashamed.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 21:5 In Thee our fathers hoped; they hoped, and Thou didst rescue them.

    Psalms 21:6 To Thee they cried out and were saved; in Thee they hoped and were not put to shame.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:11

For the Lord is compassionate and merciful; He forgives sins and saves in a time of affliction.

  1. Psalms 102:8-10 Compassion and forgiveness

    Sirach explains trust through the Lord's compassion and forgiveness; the psalm calls Him compassionate and merciful and says He has not repaid people according to their sins.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 102:8 The Lord is compassionate and merciful, patient and abundant in mercy.

    Psalms 102:9 He will not be angry to the end, nor maintain wrath for ever.

    Psalms 102:10 He has not dealt with us according to our sins, nor repaid us according to our lawless deeds,

Sirach 2:12

Woe to cowardly hearts and slack hands, and to the sinner who walks on two paths!

  1. James 1:6-8 Divided allegiance and instability

    Sirach condemns a sinner walking two paths; James describes the person of divided mind as unstable in all his ways. Both use divided direction to depict failed trust.

    Read matching passage

    James 1:6 But let him ask in faith, doubting nothing, for the one who doubts is like a wave of the sea, driven and tossed by the wind.

    James 1:7 For that person must not suppose that he will receive anything from the Lord,

    James 1:8 a man of divided mind, unstable in all his ways.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:13

Woe to the slack heart, because it does not trust; therefore it will not be sheltered.

  1. Hebrews 10:38-39 Faithlessness and lost protection

    Sirach's slack heart lacks trust and shelter; Hebrews contrasts faithful preservation of the soul with drawing back to destruction. The warnings connect abandoned trust with peril.

    Read matching passage

    Hebrews 10:38 But My righteous one will live by faith; and if he draws back, My soul takes no pleasure in him.”

    Hebrews 10:39 But we are not people who draw back to destruction, but people of faith for the preservation of the soul.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:14

Woe to you who have lost endurance! What will you do when the Lord visits?

  1. Hebrews 10:35-39 The need for endurance

    Sirach warns those who lose endurance before the Lord's visitation; Hebrews urges endurance to receive the promise and warns against drawing back while awaiting the coming One.

    Read matching passage

    Hebrews 10:35 Therefore do not throw away your confidence, which has a great reward.

    Hebrews 10:36 For you need endurance, so that, having done the will of God, you may receive the promise.

    Hebrews 10:37 “For yet a very little while, the coming One will come and will not delay.

    Hebrews 10:38 But My righteous one will live by faith; and if he draws back, My soul takes no pleasure in him.”

    Hebrews 10:39 But we are not people who draw back to destruction, but people of faith for the preservation of the soul.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:15

Those who fear the Lord will not disobey His words, and those who love Him will keep His ways.

  1. John 14:21 Love expressed in obedience

    Sirach identifies love for the Lord with keeping His ways; Jesus identifies the person keeping His commandments as the one who loves Him. Both make obedience the expression of love.

    Read matching passage

    John 14:21 The one who has My commandments and keeps them is the one who loves Me. And the one who loves Me will be loved by My Father, and I will love him and reveal Myself to him.”

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:16

Those who fear the Lord will seek His good pleasure, and those who love Him will be filled with the law.

  1. Psalms 118:47-48 Love directed toward God's law

    Sirach says those loving the Lord are filled with the law; the psalm's speaker loves, meditates on and raises his hands toward God's commandments. Both depict affectionate engagement with divine instruction.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 118:47 And I was meditating on Thy commandments, which I loved greatly.

    Psalms 118:48 And I raised my hands toward Thy commandments, which I loved, and I was pondering Thine ordinances.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:17

Those who fear the Lord will prepare their hearts and humble their souls before Him.

  1. James 4:8-10 Preparing and humbling the heart

    Sirach's preparation of hearts and humbling of souls corresponds to James' commands to purify hearts and humble oneself before the Lord.

    Read matching passage

    James 4:8 Draw near to God, and He will draw near to you. Cleanse your hands, sinners, and purify your hearts, you of divided mind.

    James 4:9 Be wretched and mourn and weep. Let your laughter be turned into mourning and your joy into dejection.

    James 4:10 Humble yourselves before the Lord, and He will exalt you.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 2:18

Let us fall into the hands of the Lord and not into human hands, for as His greatness is, so also is His mercy.

  1. 2 Samuel 24:14 Close verbal parallel

    Sirach prefers falling into the Lord's hands because of His mercy; David likewise chooses the Lord's hand rather than human hands because His compassions are many.

    Read matching passage

    2 Samuel 24:14 David said to Gad, “I am greatly hemmed in on every side. I will fall into the Lord’s hand, for His compassions are very many; I certainly will not fall into human hands.” David chose death for himself.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 3:1

Listen to me, your father, children, and act accordingly, so that you may be saved.

  1. Proverbs 4:1-4 Paternal instruction formula

    Sirach addresses children as a father asking them to listen and act; Proverbs likewise calls children to hear a father's discipline and retain his teaching.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 4:1 Listen, children, to a father’s discipline; pay attention to gain discernment,

    Proverbs 4:2 for I give you a good gift; do not abandon my law.

    Sirach commands withdrawal from injustice; Proverbs calls life's paths those turning aside from evils and says guarding one's ways preserves life. Both connect avoidance with protection.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 16:17 Paths of life turn aside from evils, and ways of righteousness are length of life. One who receives discipline will be amid good things, and one who keeps rebukes will be made wise. One who guards his own ways keeps his own life, and one who loves his life will spare his mouth.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 18:10 “Two men went up into the temple to pray, one a Pharisee and the other a tax collector.

    Luke 18:11 The Pharisee stood and prayed these things to himself: ‘God, I thank Thee that I am not like the rest of humanity—plunderers, unrighteous people, adulterers—or even like this tax collector.

    Luke 18:12 I fast twice a week; I tithe everything I acquire.’

    Luke 18:13 But the tax collector, standing far away, would not even lift his eyes to heaven, but beat his breast, saying, ‘God, be merciful to me, the sinner.’

    Luke 18:14 I tell you, this man went down to his house justified rather than the other. For everyone who exalts himself will be humbled, but the one who humbles himself will be exalted.”

    Sirach rejects counsel with a fool who cannot conceal a word; Proverbs contrasts a double-tongued revealer of plans with a faithful person who conceals matters.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 11:13 A double-tongued man reveals plans in a council, but one faithful in breath conceals matters.

  1. Micah 7:5 Limits on entrusting private matters

Sirach 9:2

Do not give your soul to a woman so that she steps upon your strength.

  1. Judges 16:15-21 narrative example of intimate disclosure followed by lost strength

    Sirach warns against surrendering oneself so that a woman overcomes one's strength; Samson's disclosure to Delilah is followed by the loss of his strength and capture, without making every relationship equivalent to that episode.

    Read matching passage

    Judges 16:15 Delilah said to him, “How can you say, ‘I love you,’ when your heart is not with me? This is the third time you have deceived me and have not told me where your great strength lies.”

    Judges 16:16 She wore him down with her words all night and troubled him until he grew weary to the point of death.

    Judges 16:17 He told her everything from his heart and said to her, “No razor shall come upon my head, for I am a Nazirite of God from my mother’s womb. If I am shaved, my strength will depart from me; I will become weak and be like everyone else.”

    Judges 16:18 Delilah saw that he had told her everything from his heart. She sent and called all the foreigners’ satraps, saying, “Come up this time, for he has told me all his heart.” All the foreigners’ satrapies came up to her, bringing the silver in their hands.

    Judges 16:19 She put him to sleep between her knees, called the barber, and had the seven curls of his head shaved. He began to be humbled, and his strength departed from him.

    Judges 16:20 Delilah said to him, “The foreigners are upon you, Samson!” He awoke from his sleep and said, “I will go out as at other times and shake myself free.” But he did not know that the Lord had departed from him.

    Judges 16:21 The foreigners seized him and dug out his eyes. They brought him down to Gaza, bound him with bronze fetters, and he ground grain in the prison house.

    Sirach calls wisdom fitting for the old and thought and counsel fitting for the honored; Elihu recounts expecting many years to bring wisdom, then qualifies this by denying that elders necessarily know judgment.

    Read matching passage

    Job 32:7 I said, ‘Time is what speaks, and in many years people know wisdom.’

    Job 32:8 But there is a spirit in mortals, and the Almighty’s breath is what teaches.

    Job 32:9 The long-lived are not wise, nor do elders know judgment.

    Matthew 5:36 Nor shall you swear by your head, for you cannot make one hair white or black.

    Matthew 5:37 But let your word be ‘Yes, yes’; ‘No, no.’ Whatever is beyond these is of evil.

Sirach 27:18

  1. 2 Samuel 1:26 narrative illustration

    Sirach compares lost friendship with bereavement. David's grief for Jonathan gives a concrete expression of mourning a beloved friend; David's loss is through death, while Sirach uses that grief as an analogy for broken trust.

Sirach 27:26

The one who digs a pit will fall into it, and the one who sets a trap will be caught in it.

  1. Ecclesiastes 10:8 literary parallel

    Both expressly say that the person who digs a pit will fall into it. Sirach adds a setter caught in his own trap, extending the pattern of the action's adverse result returning on the actor. Ecclesiastes' surrounding examples also concern work hazards, so malicious intention is not assigned to its pit digger.

    Read matching passage

    Ecclesiastes 10:8 Whoever digs a pit will fall into it, and whoever tears down a fence will be bitten by a snake.

    Sirach commends the good man who stands surety for his neighbor. Judah's surety for Benjamin becomes an offer to remain enslaved in his place, a concrete instance of accepting another's liability.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 44:32 “For your servant became surety for the lad to his father, saying, ‘If I do not bring him to you and set him before you, I shall bear the guilt before my father all my days.’

    Genesis 44:33 “Now therefore I will remain with you as a servant in the lad’s place, a household slave to my lord; but let the lad go up with his brothers.

    Genesis 44:34 “For how shall I go up to my father if the lad is not with us? Let me not see the evils which shall befall my father.”

Sirach 33:13

Like a potter’s clay in his hand—all its ways are according to his pleasure—so are human beings in the hand of their Maker, for Him to repay them according to His judgment.

  1. Romans 9:20-21 shared imagery

    Both compare the Maker's authority over people with a potter's authority over clay. Sirach emphasizes repayment according to judgment; Romans illustrates differing vessels made from the same lump for honor or dishonor.

    Read matching passage

    Romans 9:20 But who are you, O man, to answer back to God? Will what is formed say to the One who formed it, “Why didst Thou make me this way?”

    Romans 9:21 Or does the potter not have authority over the clay, from the same lump to make one vessel for honor and another for dishonor?

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:14

Good stands opposite evil, and life opposite death; so a sinner stands opposite a godly person.

  1. Deuteronomy 30:15 literary parallel

    Sirach sets good against evil and life against death before contrasting sinner and godly person. Deuteronomy explicitly places the same two pairs, life and death and good and evil, before its hearers.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 30:15 Behold, I have set before your face this day life and death, good and evil.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:15

Look in this way at all the Most High’s works: two by two, one opposite the other.

  1. Sirach 42:24-25 literary parallel

    Both passages describe the Most High's works as paired, one opposite another. Chapter 42 adds that nothing is deficient and that one confirms the other's good, elaborating the ordered pairing named in chapter 33.

    Read matching passage

    Sirach 42:24 All things are paired, one opposite another, and He has made nothing deficient.

    Sirach 42:25 One has confirmed the good things of another. Who will be filled with beholding His glory?

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:16

I too, the last, kept watch like someone gleaning behind grape-gatherers.

  1. Ecclesiastes 12:9-10 authorial comparison

    Within Sirach 33:16-18 the late gleaner image describes gathering instruction for others. Ecclesiastes likewise describes a wise teacher teaching the people knowledge and seeking truthful written words; the agricultural image belongs only to Sirach.

    Read matching passage

    Ecclesiastes 12:9 Besides becoming wise, Ecclesiastes also taught the people knowledge; and an ear will trace out an orderly arrangement of proverbs.

    Ecclesiastes 12:10 Ecclesiastes sought much to find words of delight and something written in uprightness, words of truth.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:17

By the Lord’s blessing I came in time, and like a grape-gatherer I filled a winepress.

  1. Ecclesiastes 12:9-11 authorial comparison

    Sirach's filled winepress continues its image of successfully gathered instruction, as clarified by 33:18. Ecclesiastes describes collected wise words, their ordered arrangement, and their gift from one shepherd; the comparison concerns the gathered teaching, not a literal harvest.

    Read matching passage

    Ecclesiastes 12:9 Besides becoming wise, Ecclesiastes also taught the people knowledge; and an ear will trace out an orderly arrangement of proverbs.

    Ecclesiastes 12:10 Ecclesiastes sought much to find words of delight and something written in uprightness, words of truth.

    Ecclesiastes 12:11 The words of the wise are like goads and like planted nails, which were given from collections, from one shepherd, and something beyond them.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:18

Consider that I have labored not for myself alone, but for all who seek instruction.

  1. Ecclesiastes 12:9 authorial parallel

    Sirach says his labor serves everyone seeking instruction rather than himself alone. Ecclesiastes likewise describes the wise author teaching the people knowledge.

    Read matching passage

    Ecclesiastes 12:9 Besides becoming wise, Ecclesiastes also taught the people knowledge; and an ear will trace out an orderly arrangement of proverbs.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:19

Listen to me, dignitaries of the people; give ear, leaders of the assembly.

  1. Wisdom of Solomon 6:1-2 literary parallel

    Both summon public leaders to listen and learn. Sirach addresses dignitaries and assembly leaders; Wisdom addresses kings, judges, and rulers of multitudes with the same call to give ear.

    Read matching passage

    Wisdom of Solomon 6:1 Listen, then, kings, and understand; learn, judges of the ends of the earth.

    Wisdom of Solomon 6:2 Give ear, you who rule a multitude and boast over crowds of nations;

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:21

As long as you still live and breath is in you, do not exchange yourself for any flesh.

  1. 1 Corinthians 7:23 ethical comparison

    Sirach warns the living person not to exchange himself for any human being. Paul tells those bought at a price not to become slaves of humans; the resistance to human subjection is shared, with Paul's distinct theological reason retained.

    Read matching passage

    1 Corinthians 7:23 You were bought at a price; do not become slaves of human beings.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:22

For it is better that your children ask you than that you look toward your sons’ hands.

  1. 2 Corinthians 12:14 social parallel

    Sirach prefers children depending on the parent to the parent depending on the children's hands. Paul likewise says parents should store up for children rather than children for parents, using that family expectation to explain his own conduct.

    Read matching passage

    2 Corinthians 12:14 Look, for the third time I am ready to come to you, and I will not be a burden. For I seek not what is yours but you; for the children ought not to store up for the parents, but the parents for the children.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:24

On the day when your life’s days are completed, at the time of death, distribute your inheritance.

  1. Hebrews 9:16-17 legal comparison

    Sirach locates inheritance distribution at the completion of life. Hebrews explains that a testament takes effect at the maker's death and is not in force during his life, connecting inheritance arrangements to that same transition.

    Read matching passage

    Hebrews 9:16 For where there is a testament, the death of the one who made it must be established.

    Hebrews 9:17 For a testament takes effect at death, since it is never in force while the one who made it lives.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:25

Fodder, a rod, and burdens for a donkey; bread, discipline, and work for a household slave.

  1. Proverbs 26:3 shared imagery

    Sirach places the donkey's rod and burdens beside the household slave's discipline and work. Proverbs compares horse and donkey restraints with a rod for a lawless nation; both ancient disciplinary analogies transfer animal-control imagery to human settings without naming the same human group.

    Read matching passage

    Proverbs 26:3 As a whip for a horse and a goad for a donkey, so a rod for a lawless nation.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:27

A yoke and strap will bend a neck, and racks and tortures are for a wrongdoing household slave.

  1. Exodus 21:20-21 legal comparison

    Sirach prescribes coercive restraints and torture for a wrongdoing household slave. Exodus addresses a master's beating of an enslaved person with a rod and distinguishes liability by the outcome. The comparison records ancient treatment and legal limits rather than equating their prescriptions.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 21:20 And if a man strikes his male slave or his female slave with a rod, and the slave dies under his hands, he shall surely be avenged by judgment.

    Exodus 21:21 But if the slave survives a day or two, he shall not be avenged; for he is his silver.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:28

Put him to work so that he is not idle, for idleness has taught much evil.

  1. 1 Timothy 5:13 ethical comparison

    Sirach associates idleness with learning evil and therefore assigns work to the servant. Paul describes certain widows learning idleness and becoming gossips and busybodies; both link inactivity with learned misconduct, while addressing different groups.

    Read matching passage

    1 Timothy 5:13 And at the same time they learn to be idle, going around the houses, and not only idle but also gossips and busybodies, saying things they ought not to say.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:29

Assign him tasks as befits him, and if he does not obey, make his fetters heavy.

  1. Ephesians 6:9 ethical contrast

    Sirach tells the master to assign tasks and increase fetters for disobedience. Ephesians addresses masters with a contrasting restraint: give up threats, remembering the impartial heavenly Master. Their distinct approaches to coercion are preserved.

    Read matching passage

    Ephesians 6:9 And masters, act in the same way toward them, giving up threats, knowing that both their Master and yours is in the heavens, and there is no partiality with Him.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:30

Do not go to excess against any flesh, and do nothing without judgment.

  1. Colossians 4:1 ethical parallel

    Within household discipline Sirach prohibits excess against any person and requires judgment. Colossians likewise requires masters to provide what is just and equitable to enslaved people, grounding that duty in their own accountability to a heavenly Master.

    Read matching passage

    Colossians 4:1 Masters, provide your slaves with what is just and equitable, knowing that you too have a Master in heaven.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:31

If you have a household slave, let him be as yourself, because you acquired him with blood.

  1. Philemon 1:16-17 ethical comparison

    Sirach tells the owner to regard the household slave as himself. Paul asks that Onesimus be received as a beloved brother and welcomed as Paul himself; both require personal or familial regard for the enslaved person, while giving distinct reasons.

    Read matching passage

    Philemon 1:16 no longer as a slave, but more than a slave, a beloved brother, especially to me, but how much more to you, both in the flesh and in the Lord.

    Philemon 1:17 So if you regard me as a partner, welcome him as you would me.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:32

If you have a household slave, treat him as a brother, because you will need him as your own soul.

  1. Philemon 1:16 ethical parallel

    Sirach explicitly requires treating the household slave as a brother. Paul likewise asks that Onesimus be received as more than a slave, a beloved brother in the flesh and in the Lord, extending the familial description in a Christian setting.

    Read matching passage

    Philemon 1:16 no longer as a slave, but more than a slave, a beloved brother, especially to me, but how much more to you, both in the flesh and in the Lord.

    Pinned source record

Sirach 33:33

If you mistreat him and he leaves and runs away, on what road will you look for him?

  1. Deuteronomy 23:16-17 legal comparison

    Sirach warns that mistreatment can make an enslaved person flee beyond recovery. Deuteronomy addresses the person who escapes a master and forbids returning or oppressing him, viewing the same flight from the receiving community's protective obligation.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 23:16 You shall not deliver unto his master a slave who has come unto you from his master.

    Deuteronomy 23:17 He shall dwell with you; he shall dwell among you in any place that pleases him. You shall not afflict him.
